Why Do Football Clubs Let Players Leave for Free?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Why would a club let a valuable player walk out the door for nothing?

Because sometimes nothing is the best offer on the table.

Football clubs may let a player leave for free when the cost or risk of a new contract outweighs the value of keeping him.

A club may also prefer one final season of sporting contribution to a modest transfer fee. Sometimes the player chooses not to renew, while the signing club can still face wages, bonuses and agent fees.

So the word “free” does a lot of heavy lifting in football.

It is the only price tag in the sport that tells you what the selling club gets, and nothing about what the buying club pays.

This guide explains the topic for senior professional football, with examples drawn from English football.

Where FIFA rules and domestic rules part ways, we say so without hesitation.

A free transfer is a move in which no transfer fee is paid to the previous club, commonly because the player’s contract has expired.

When we’re talking about a free agent, we’re talking about an unattached player.

“Free” describes the absence of a transfer fee here. It does not remove wages, signing costs or any compensation that rules may still require.

The word also gets stretched quite a bit.

A contract that runs out on its end date is a free transfer. A deal that both sides agree to end early, called mutual termination, is something else. So is a release, where a club lets a player go while he is still under contract.

Headlines rarely make the distinction, but the rulebook does.

You can compare free signings with fee-paying moves in our confirmed football transfers database.

No sporting director wakes up planning to lose a player for nothing.

It happens when every other option looks worse, which in football is more often than fans like to believe.

Unless a club explains its motive in public (and clubs rarely do), read them as general reasoning, not as the known story behind any specific deal.

A renewal is rarely a copy of the old deal.

It usually comes with a raise, a signing bonus and a fresh agent fee. If the club doubts the player will be worth that money in year three or four, letting the contract expire is the cheaper mistake.

A lengthy contract for an older player can increase a club’s exposure to injury or declining performance.

The club pays the full salary whether the player stays fit or not, and many clubs would rather stop early.

A player who no longer fits still collects his wages, and those wages still count against the club’s spending limits. Loyalty does not have its own line in the accounts.

Sometimes a club lets a contract run out on purpose.

If the best offer is modest, one more season of goals, a title push or a cup run can be worth more than the money.

Fans may call it a blunder in January. By May, some of them call it a trophy.

A club cannot force a renewal and it cannot force a sale either.

If the player turns down every new contract and every move his club agrees, the deal simply runs out. He leaves for free, usually with a better offer already waiting somewhere else.

A short contract gives a club less long-term wage exposure and gives a player an earlier chance to renegotiate or move. It also offers less security to the player and less protection of resale value to the club. The right length depends on performance risk, bargaining power and each side’s plans.

For a club, a two-year deal limits the damage if a signing flops. The price is that the club may be shopping for a replacement sooner than planned.

It protects resale value, because any buyer must pay to take over the years still left. But it turns into an anchor if the player’s form drops, since the wages keep coming whether he plays or not.

Short contracts and free transfers are related, but they are not the same decision.

A short deal moves the end date closer. What happens on that date is a separate argument, usually held in a room full of agents.

Every contract has a clock on it, and the clock usually works for the player.

As a contract approaches expiry, a club will generally have less leverage in transfer negotiations, because the player may become available without a fee.

Is there a formula for how fast that value falls?

Form, demand, injuries and competition among interested clubs can all affect the fee available.

For clubs hoping to recover a transfer fee, an earlier sale or a contract extension may improve their position.

Keeping the player until expiry can still be the better sporting decision.

A player’s value and the cost of signing him are not necessarily the same thing. Transfer fees attract most of the headlines, while wages, signing bonuses and agent payments can be just as important to a club’s finances. Transfer speculation also extends beyond traditional football reporting, with discussions appearing on social media and platforms such as crypto betting site Sportbet.one, although these conversations should not be confused with verified transfer fees or the actual financial terms of a deal.

Before 1995, a player at the end of his contract was not really free.

His old club could still demand a fee before letting him go, and Jean-Marc Bosman, a Belgian midfielder, found that out in 1990.

He was nearing the end of his contract with RFC Liège and rejected a new offer that would have cut his salary by about 75%. He wanted to join French club Dunkerque instead.

The two clubs reached a conditional transfer agreement, but Liège doubted Dunkerque’s ability to pay and never requested the transfer certificate the move required. The deal fell through, and Liège suspended him.

He won five years later, and thousands of players have earned more from his case than he ever did.

The Bosman ruling of December 15, 1995 stopped clubs from demanding a fee for an out-of-contract player moving to a club in another EU member state.

It did not end every form of post-contract compensation worldwide, whatever the legend says.

When it comes to signings, FIFA’s rules set the timing.

Under article 18(3) of the FIFA Regulations on the Status and Transfer of Players (July 2025 edition), a player may sign with a new club once his contract has expired or is due to expire within six months.

The new club must first tell the current club in writing.

The six months count back from the real end date, so the magic month is not always January.

And signing a future contract is not the same as being registered to play.

Registration still depends on the transfer window and domestic rules.

The signing club may pay wages, a signing-on bonus and agent fees, even when no transfer fee goes to the previous club.

Compare the full contract period and separate one-off payments from annual pay before judging which deal costs less.

Illustrative five-year signing costs. Original SoccerNews editorial example using hypothetical figures, not a real transfer or market average.

Calculation: fee-paying = £20m + (5 × £3m) + £1m + £2m = £38m. Out-of-contract = £0 + (5 × £5m) + £5m + £3m = £33m. Annual wages are counted once, through the five-year total. Assumptions: both players complete five full years; wages are fixed gross amounts; bonuses and agent fees are paid by the signing club and counted once. Excludes employer payroll charges, taxes, performance bonuses, financing, add-ons, training compensation, resale income and early termination. These are nominal commitments, not cash schedules or accounting expenses.

In this hypothetical example, the free signing costs £33m over five years, compared with £38m for the fee-paying signing.

Higher wages and signing costs absorb much of the saved fee. Different contract terms could reverse the result.

In other words, the costs shift between transfer fees, wages and other signing expenses.

Which brings us to the most confusing pair of words in football. A free agent is the player. A football agent is the representative who negotiates for him, and sometimes for a club. Only one of those job titles contains the word “free.” Neither of them works for free.

Premier League clubs reported £460.3 million in payments to football agents for the period from February 4, 2025 to February 2, 2026, according to The FA.

The figure covers reported agent payments across different types of transactions, including some agreed before the reporting period. It should not be read as the cost of free transfers alone.

Premier League Squad Cost Ratio (SCR) rules in force for 2026/27 assess defined squad costs against relevant revenue and player-sale results.

The 85% Green Threshold operates alongside additional allowance and a Red Threshold, so crossing 85% does not automatically trigger a points deduction. Free signings still create relevant costs through pay and other deal expenses. (Premier League)

In plain terms, every pound spent on the squad now fights for the same space.

Squad costs include wages, agent fees and transfer fees spread over time, a process called amortization. Since the Premier League’s five-year rule for new or extended contracts, a fee can be spread over five years at most.

So a £20m fee on a five-year deal adds £4m a year to the accounts (£20m ÷ 5). This assumes straight-line accounting and no early sale or write-down. It is an accounting charge, not the cash payment, which can follow a different schedule.

In this example, an out-of-contract signing avoids the £4m annual amortization charge linked to the hypothetical £20m transfer fee. The club must still account for the player’s wages and any other signing or registration costs. That is the part of “free” the unveiling photo never shows.

You can follow how this plays out window by window in our Premier League transfers coverage.

A club that lets a player walk is not always being careless.

It weighs sporting value against the price of a renewal, and the player gets a vote too. For the signing club, “free” generally means no transfer fee to the previous club, not a cost-free deal.

Under FIFA’s July 2025 regulations, a professional may conclude a contract with another club when his current contract has expired or is due to expire within six months. The approaching club must first inform the current club in writing. Domestic rules and the regulatory edition in force must also be checked.

Some out-of-contract players can be registered outside a registration period under specified exceptions. Being unattached does not create an unlimited right to register at any time. The date the contract ended, association rules and competition eligibility all matter.

No transfer fee does not always mean no compensation. Training-compensation rules can apply to qualifying young players, and domestic schemes may also apply. Contract expiry is different from early termination, which can raise other compensation questions.

It is a one-off payment a club makes to a player for agreeing to join. A signing-on bonus may be substantial in a free transfer, but the amount depends on the negotiation.

No. A club can accept an offer, but the player must agree personal terms with the buyer. If he says no, he stays until his contract ends.

Rule summaries are based on the Bosman judgment (C-415/93), the FIFA Regulations on the Status and Transfer of Players (July 2025 edition), Premier League statements on squad-cost rules and amortization, and The FA’s 2025-26 agent-payment report. The cost table is an original SoccerNews example with hypothetical figures. Rules change, so check the edition in force on the date you read this. Browse more football explainers.

Kim Kardashian's shocking advent calendar is the perfect metaphor for 2026

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Christmas is coming, which means annoying music in shops, an inevitable lack of snow, and Kim Kardashian charging $148 for the contents of her bathroom drawer.

Kim's 'SKIMS Accessories Advent Calendar' has achieved that rare festive miracle: making a chocolate-based advent calendar look like a sound financial investment. Because what we have here, for a cool $148/£158, is twelve days of tat. Hair ties. Scrunchies. Bobby pins. Keychains. A squishy toy. Something called a 'bra elastic headband'. It's essentially the contents of a lost property box.

Unsurprisingly, the internet is having a field day. A viral TikTok unboxing has attracted tens of millions of views, with its creator becoming visibly more exasperated as each compartment reveals another object you could reasonably expect to find down the back of a sofa, culminating in the grand finale: a furry compact mirror keychain.

It's worth saying that from a branding packaging design perspective, it's almost impressive. It's a nice-looking object with pretty illustrations, and the SKIMS wordmark is striking in a bold, festive red. But there's something beautifully 2026 about taking a handful of mundane objects, putting them in expensive-looking packaging and asking people to cough up the cost of an actual spa day. Like so many of this year's main characters (AI, subscription-based everything, $10 matcha lattes), it's overpriced, overhyped and underwhelming. But unlike AI, that $148 leaving your bank account definitely isn't artificial.

In response to the backlash, SKIMS has emailed customers who purchased the advent calendar: “We wanted to reach out personally about your recent purchase of the Holiday 2026 advent calendar. We understand the product did not meet customers’ expectations, and we are truly sorry. We want every customer to be happy with what they receive, and we fell short this time.”

Believe it or not, this isn't the first time we've seen a celebrity's brand take a hit as a result of an underwhelming advent calendar. Back in 2017, popular vlogger Zoella faced backlash for her own calendar. Like Kim's it contained only twelve doors, all of which were filled with items that might be found down the back of a sofa. Unlike Kim's, it was a mere $60. Inflation, eh?

If you need help restoring your faith in the festive season, check out the magical Disney Holiday Short, which is moving people to tears.

Microsoft-Backed Space and Time Launches Platform to Let AI Agents Verify Private Data

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Microsoft-Backed Space and Time Launches Platform to Let AI Agents Verify Private Data

Space and Time, a Microsoft-backed blockchain data project, has launched a platform designed to let businesses use private data in AI tools without handing over the underlying records, the company announced on Monday.

Space and Time Enterprise lets companies prove that calculations made on their private data are correct, so that AI applications, auditors and other stakeholders can check the results without accessing the database itself. Launch partners include onchain asset manager Maple Finance, Indonesian vehicle maker and distributor Indomobil, Oracle software consultancy Trenez and AI data network The Data Foundation.

"Businesses want AI agents to execute workflows using that information, but those agents don't need unrestricted access to the records behind every calculation," Nate Holiday, CEO of MakeInfinite Labs, the original developer of Space and Time, told Decrypt.

The stakes are high, he argued. "The potential cost to a business is the overall value of the business," Holiday said. "Giving AI tools unrestricted access to data creates another avenue of exposure."

Holiday said the company is working with customers in industries including construction, engineering and manufacturing that want to rebuild their systems around AI, so that agents, reporting tools and compliance teams can work from the same business records and check the calculations they rely on.

The platform is built on Proof of SQL, Space and Time's patented technology for proving that database queries were computed correctly. It already works with Google Cloud's BigQuery, and businesses can now run it on their own servers without operating a blockchain, buying specialized hardware or relying on an outside proving service, according to the company.

With GPU acceleration, supported queries over millions of rows can be run and proven in under a second, Holiday said, though actual performance depends on the query, hardware and dataset.

The technology proves that a calculation was carried out correctly on a given set of records, but it cannot tell whether those records were accurate to begin with. "An incorrect entry still needs to be caught through the business's data controls," Holiday said, "but verification gives those controls a much stronger foundation: evidence of which data was used and whether it was processed correctly."

Space and Time raised $20 million in 2022 in a strategic funding round led by M12, Microsoft's venture fund. Last year, Grayscale launched a trust giving investors exposure to SXT, the token that powers Space and Time's blockchain. Earlier this year, the company launched Dreamspace, an AI-powered tool built with Microsoft's Azure AI services that lets users create and deploy onchain apps without writing code.

Space and Time also runs SXT Chain, a decentralized network that supplies verifiable data to DeFi, tokenized assets and stablecoins. Asked whether enterprise customers would create demand for SXT, Holiday said businesses could deploy the product in their own environment, and that the network would come into play as their applications "need to connect to onchain markets," with those workloads paid for through Space and Time.

The company's cryptography is also used elsewhere. Microsoft's Nova project includes Blitzar, Space and Time's cryptographic acceleration library, and the team's work on a commitment scheme called Dory contributed to a16z crypto's Jolt proof system, Holiday said.

‘Henry’s Ebb’ Is a Haunting, Horror-Tinged Debut Film With a ‘Widow’s Bay’ Star, an Imaginary Friend — and Much to Unpeel

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Writer-director Thomas Mendolia discusses his road movie, set in the American South and premiering at Tallinn, how he cast stars Kingston Rumi Southwick and Banks Repeta, and his love for Jeff Nichols' films.

Henry’s Ebb, the debut feature film from writer-director Thomas Mendolia, invites you to travel to the Southeastern United States in the 1990s. But don’t expect too much Southern hospitality! Henry loves his best friend Ebb and the fearlessness he brings to the table. And he is always there for him, especially when Henry’s struggling and unpredictable single mother turns abusive. So, the two friends decide to set off to find Henry’s father, whom he barely remembers, in hopes of a different life.

But Ebb is not only protective but also manipulative. As the two boys travel through the American South and meet different people, their bond slowly starts fraying, and their priorities diverge more and more. The tension between them is particularly painful because Ebb helped Henry survive a tumultuous childhood. Oh yeah, and did we mention that Ebb is an imaginary friend? Related Stories Movies Watch Disney's Holiday Short 'The Invisible Horse' From 'Paddington in Peru' Director Dougal Wilson Movies 'A Photographer in Cannes' Follows an Estonian Who Knows the World's Red (and Black) Carpets - and Loved Getting a Vin Diesel Fist Bump

A journey of longing that kicks off as a drama- and trauma-fueled adventure — think Tom Sawyer and Huckleberry Finn! — through beautiful Southern landscapes soon reveals a darker and psychological horror side.

Widow’s Bay star Kingston Rumi Southwick plays Henry, while Banks Repeta (Armageddon Time) portrays Ebb. Ashley Shelton and Will Holland are also part of the cast. Henry’s Ebb features cinematography by Chaochen Li and editing by Tyler Ten Haken. The producers are Lucas Ford and Joey LePage, with co-producers Tony Tehle and Irina Slepneva. Bridge Independent is handling sales.

For its world premiere on Wednesday, Nov. 11, Henry’s Ebb is traveling from the American South to cold Estonia, where it debuts in the 26th edition of the Youth and Children’s Film Festival, part of the 30th edition of the Tallinn Black Nights Film Festival (PÖFF) in Estonia.

Mendolia talked to THR about the inspirations for Henry’s Ebb, bringing together different genres and moods, his love for Jeff Nichols, and whether we may see more features from him.

There are all sorts of layers that we slowly discover and have to peel back in Henry’s Ebb, and some that are only hinted at. What inspired this film, and did you study psychology or how did you get inside your characters’ minds and hearts?

No, I did not study psychology. I actually was an acting major in my undergrad. But I’ve always enjoyed watching people. It is just fascinating why people choose to be a certain way or do certain things.

This movie started with this image of two boys on a bike going through the American South. This was one of those 2 a.m. ideas where you can’t sleep. And I wondered who these boys are, where they are going, and what’s happening. And then it was just: What if one of them is imaginary? We’ve all seen Fight Club, and we’ve all seen A Beautiful Mind. We’ve seen these movies where the imaginary friend is revealed at the end. So, I thought, what if we rip that band-aid off in the very beginning and we explore the reasoning, the why? Why does Henry have an imaginary friend? Why does he need one? Can he get rid of it?

I came away with a feeling for why Henry needs Ebb. Anything you can share on that?

This is a boy at the end of his youth who is growing into a man, growing into an adult and may have to shed some of those childhood coping mechanisms that he’s created for himself.

I heard you were born in New York, but your family moved to the South when you were younger. Did you base any locations on that childhood experience?

Yes, I was born in Brooklyn to an Italian-American family, but I was raised in the American South. As I was writing, what just flowed out of me was this hyper-nostalgia. It was almost like diving into my past. I wrote all the locations that I grew up in as a kid — all from memory, and I hadn’t been to those places in a long time. This was the ’90s, so a fun game was us running through the forest. And the forest is such an oddly magical, dark, mysterious, but also warm and comforting place.

Writing the film was like the road movies that I loved growing up. It just unfolded with a lot of personal, emotional baggage that was undeniably infused into this movie. I don’t mean 100 percent. My mother is a very lovely person. But there were all sorts of emotions and feelings I was going through at the time of writing this movie. It came out of something deeply personal.

I don’t want to go through Henry’s experiences, but I loved the nature, small towns and other locations you take us to, especially because I have never been there.

I’m glad that you felt that way. Never going to this place, but feeling connected to it is super important to what I wanted to do with this film. Some of the best films that I loved growing up have this hyper- localization, hyper-specificity of a region — films like Fargo. And one of my favorite filmmakers, who influences me all the time, is Jeff Nichols, who is constantly filming in the American South.

I was raised in a town called Elkin in the Appalachian region of North Carolina, and it’s very specific. And that’s exactly what I wanted to capture. I believe that the more focused, the more specific, I can get with my voice or the things that I say in a film, the more universal it becomes. I think that is beautiful and why we make movies. They connect us all around the globe. And it’s so fascinating that this film is premiering on the opposite side of the planet.

How did you approach the genre elements of Henry’s Ebb? There are certain situations where they come into the foreground, and it felt like you chose those moments deliberately. Does that make sense?

Yeah, 100 percent. I’ve always loved genre. I’ve always loved horror. I’ve always thought that it was such a beautiful platform to experience the human psyche, and it’s constantly evolving, as opposed to other genres. It’s so malleable, and you can do it with a lower budget, but it can also enhance the themes that you want to delve into. You can blow them up a little bit.

When you look at Henry’s fear of growing up and his need to escape, Ebb is a visual manifestation of his coping mechanism. Ebb represents the thing that Henry needs to do to feel safe. We as humans create these coping mechanisms to get us through life. But at a certain point, they can hinder us from growing into a bigger person, and this is Henry’s journey.

The horror element allowed us to keep pushing boundaries. We wanted to be very clear that any time there was a horror incident or a big set piece, it was not just for scares. It all had to be very purposeful regarding what Henry is scared of in this particular moment. This may sound like a silly comparison, but in Cloverfield, the monster, and how Matt Reeves talks about it, is this terrified baby. Ebb is not a baby, but Ebb is scared of losing Henry and scared of what the future could be. Everything that Ebb does is out of fear, and horror can enhance all of that.

How did you find your two young leads? Kingston is now widely known thanks to Widow’s Bay, and Banks was in Armageddon Time, and they have great chemistry…

I saw Banks’ Henry audition and felt he’s done Armageddon Time, which is in itself a nostalgic film based on the director [James Gray’s] childhood, so he gets this idea. And then he sent in an audition for Ebb, which was so fascinating because it was very vulnerable. A lot of the auditions were like, “I’m a bad guy, and I know I’m bad and abrasive, and I can say swear words.” But Banks’ audition had this vulnerability underneath. And he is from Wilmington and lives in North Carolina, so he’s local to the area in the film.

With Henry, I was very scared because this film hangs on the back of a younger actor, so we talked about a younger person who needs to act a little bit mature, but is also still a kid. And when Kingston’s audition came in, it was immediate. His tape was incredible. And when we talked over Zoom, we just talked about the script. There was so much that I didn’t even [spell out] in the script that he just got. He’s naturally a more mature person and a more vulnerable person, and you definitely see that on screen.

And putting Kingston and Banks together was so fascinating because they are such different people, but they have this chemistry. They are like Sherlock and Watson. They were perfect for Henry and Ebb.

I do have two to three big ideas, but there is one that I have talked to Lucas about, who is a producer on Henry’s Ebb as well.

I recall he worked in production management on Backrooms?

Anything you can share about this project?

I won’t say the title, and I won’t go too far into detail, but I’ll give you the broad strokes. We were working on this before Henry’s Ebb. He gave me this book; I looked at the back and felt: “This has me written all over it.” Then I read it, and I realized it was a non-fiction book about a real place. Fascinating!

So I had this idea for what the movie that I thought this book was going to actually be would look like. It is in the genre world. It is definitely a lot darker. It’s like Prisoners meets Ex Machina. There is a father looking for his son. It’s definitely a story about a family. I tend to go in that direction. It is a father-and-son reconnecting story set in the Appalachian area, and there is a sci-fi element to it. It’s a very dark version of the Spielberg movies that I grew up loving.

The Best Ski Gear for Powder Days and Storm Chasing

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

Chasing powder this winter? These tester-favorite skis, boots, shell, goggles and avalanche gear keep you dry, safe and skiing deep all storm cycle long. (Photo: Ray J Gadd)

Storm chasing is what most skiers dream about. One minute you’re scrolling the forecast from your desk, the next you’re cramming gear into a bag and packing up the car or hopping on a flight to score some freshies. It’s how we’d all love to spend our winters, sniffing out the best forecasts and bouncing between deep days around the country. With a strong, possibly historic El Niño predicted for this winter, there’s no telling exactly where the storm track will decide to unload. Nailing a good storm cycle takes patience, a little planning and a willingness to drop everything and go when the conditions allow. That, and the right gear to stay dry and comfortable all day long on the mountain—nothing kills a day faster than soaking wet mittens or fogged-up goggles.

Here’s the ski setup, storm-ready layers, and safety essentials our testers have long counted on for traveling far and wide to chase the biggest storms—plus a rolling bag to get it all there. Because the more dialed your gear is, the more time you can spend hunting for powder stashes. Pair these gear recs with SKI’s 2027 Resort Guide, the best tool for getting familiar with resorts across North America. That way, you’ll have a shortlist of options ready when the snow starts flying, wherever El Niño sends it.

If you have to choose just one ski for a powder-hunting mission, the Salomon S/Lab QST X is our top choice. With a 116-millimeter waist and freeride rocker profile, these sticks were made to float through champagne powder as well as dense, surfy snow, with a caruba and poplar wood core that’s light and lively. Flax and carbon fibers plus cork in the tip and tail keep things smooth and stable—a ski that can stand up to hard-charging freeriders but remains accessible for intermediates. Its midweight design—2,000 grams per ski at the 184 centimeter length—allows skiers to ride all day inbounds or mount up a hybrid binding for dropping out of bounds.

Even on a resort trip, it’s nice to keep your options open. If your plan is to primarily ski inbounds with a few short tours, the Salomon S/Lab Shift 2 10 MN Bindings are your ticket to ultimate flexibility. Salomon redesigned the Shift bindings this year, beefing up the construction in the heel and toe to help prevent pre-release and boost durability. On snow, most skiers won’t notice a big sacrifice compared with a traditional alpine binding, and you get the added benefit of a tour-capable binding perfect for a quick tour when the terrain across the valley beckons.

No one wants to tote around a second pair of boots on a ski trip. Tecnica’s new Zero G Decoy Pro boots are powerful hybrids, an ideal one-boot solution for deep trips. The Decoy delivers solid inbounds performance thanks to a stiff 130 flex, and the walk-mode lever is easy to flip with gloves on, making it simple to power up a bootpack or skintrack—or just cruise around the base area without killing your feet.

For deep days inbounds and in the backcountry, the 3-layer PowSlayer jacket and pants—available in men’s and women’s—provide ample protection without weighing you down. Powder days are full of temperature swings, and the PowSlayer kit uses a Gore-Tex ePE membrane, which repels moisture while regulating your body temp as you transition from frigid summits to top-to-bottom runs. The jacket sports a cinchable storm hood for blustery lift rides, and the pants feature a sewn-in pocket for stowing a transceiver securely.

Visibility is often limited on the deep days we dream of, and high-performance goggles become more crucial than ever. Smith’s 4D MAG ChromaPop Goggles with an extra lens offer a large field of view and the option of a photochromic lens, which is ideal on days when the light flows in and out as a storm moves through. Lens swapping is easy with these—a magnetic system lets you pop lenses in and out during a quick gondola ride.

For deep days when it feels like fresh snow is sneaking into every little crevice, cinch up Black Diamond’s Mercury Mittens and consider the problem solved. A toasty fleece-lined gauntlet design delivers ample coverage while you explore the white room, and a waterproof BD.dry insert keeps your hands dry all day long. Synthetic insulation is low-profile, and the 4-way stretch shell provides a comfortable fit that lets you grip your poles securely.

An extra neckwarmer is crucial for storm skiing, and having a dry one on hand to swap in midday can easily add a few extra hours to your day. Skida’s Alpine Neckwarmer provides cozy relief from frigid wind, with a stretchy polyester shell that sheds moisture, and a fleece lining that’s like a hug for your face. The Alpine Neckwarmer is ideal for those uber-chilly powder days, while a lighter option such as the Snow Tour can be great for warmer PNW storms.

Low-profile yet spacious, the Dakine Heli Pro 20L Backpack is compact enough to ride chairs all day, with enough space for shorter ski tours and out-of-bounds laps. There’s a dedicated safety pocket to stash your shovel and probe, as well as a fleece-lined goggle pocket to protect your goggles while you hike, or keep an extra lens on hand for fogging emergencies. There are both diagonal and A-frame carry options, so it’s easy to stow your skis for quick bootpacks.

A beacon is a necessity for skiing out of bounds, and it’s never a bad idea to switch yours on for deep days at the resort as well. The industry-leading beacon for professionals and advanced skiers alike, Mammut’s Barryvox S2 Beacon features all the bells and whistles you’d want out of a pro-level device: Group Check modes, an advanced flagging feature and a 70-meter range. Recent updates include a high-contrast display and Rescue Send, which switches itself back into send mode if you’ve been still for four minutes or more.

Hauling your gear for a ski trip can feel like a hassle, and the Backcountry BGA Cottonwood Double Ski and Snowboard Rolling Bag makes the process smoother. With a unique roll-top design, the padded bag can hold skis up to 195 centimeters—but easily rolls smaller if you don’t need the space—and internal and external storage pockets are perfect for stashing dirty socks for the way home. It’s spacious enough to fit two pairs of skis plus ample gear, and the durable 840-denier polyester and nylon construction stands up to years of wear and tear.

For Every Condo Buyer in the U.S., There Are More Than Two Sellers. That Gives Condo Buyers Strong Negotiating Power.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Condo buyers today have more negotiating power than they’ve had in at least 13 years. There were an estimated 123% more condo sellers than buyers nationwide in September, the largest gap in records dating back to 2013. The gap widened from 95.5% a year earlier.

Redfin estimates the number of buyers using proprietary data on the typical time between a buyer’s first home tour and the closing of a purchase, along with MLS data on active listings and pending sales. The estimated number of sellers is based on active listings in the MLS. The figures in this report are national estimates. See the full methodology and explore the interactive dashboard.

Redfin defines a buyer’s market as one with more than 10% more sellers than buyers, and a seller’s market as one with more than 10% more buyers than sellers. A market is considered balanced when the difference between sellers and buyers is within 10%. Other housing-market statistics, including median sale price, are based on Redfin’s analysis of MLS data.

A buyer’s market doesn’t necessarily mean condos are affordable for buyers.

The condo market has undergone a dramatic reversal since the pandemic-era homebuying boom. In September 2021, there were 25.1% fewer sellers than buyers.

Five years later, the number of buyers fell 54%, while the number of sellers rose 36%. The shift has left sellers competing for a much smaller pool of potential buyers.

The number of condo buyers fell to an estimated 88,460 in September, down 2.5 % from August and the lowest level in at least 13 years. Meanwhile, condo sellers totaled 197,248, up 0.9 % from the prior month.

Several factors are weighing more heavily on the condo market than on the single-family home market. Rising insurance premiums, HOA fees and special assessments are making condos increasingly expensive to own, particularly in Florida and Texas, where hurricane risks and other natural disasters have contributed to higher insurance costs. That’s a problem for first-time buyers, who often turn to condos as a more affordable entry point into the housing market but lack equity from a previous home sale.

While the condo market is challenging for buyers, those who can afford housing costs will have more power in negotiations.

“For renters who have saved for a down payment and are ready to build equity, these buyer-friendly conditions are an opportunity,” said Asad Khan, a senior economist at Redfin. “Buyers have more options and a better chance of finding a place they like. Although condo prices and HOA fees remain relatively high, buyers have more room to negotiate price cuts, and condos are still generally more affordable than single-family homes.”

The single-family market saw a similar trend in September, with the number of buyers dropping to a record low since 2013. Sellers outnumbered buyers by 53.7%–a wide gap, but less than half as wide as what we’re seeing in the condo market.

The huge surplus of condo sellers over buyers is contributing to the record-breaking buyer’s market in the overall housing market, which encompasses condos, townhouses and single-family homes

The national median condo sale price fell 0.8% to about $339,000 in September, the first year-over-year decline in five months.

House hunters should keep in mind that the costs of buying and owning a condo extend beyond its purchase price, and those ongoing expenses can put additional pressure on buyers’ budgets. Rising homeowners association (HOA) fees and insurance premiums can push up the total monthly cost of ownership. Buyers should also consider the possibility of special assessments to cover major repairs or improvements to a building’s common areas.

But for buyers who can afford the upfront costs of a condo and plan to stay put for several years, today’s buyer’s  market may offer an opportunity.

“Find a motivated seller and there are a lot of them,” said Cecilia Cordova, a Redfin Premier agent in Miami, who lives in a condominium herself. Researching the buildings you’d like to live in is the key to finding a financially healthy community and avoiding surprise assessment bills later, she said.

Condo buyers have more options than they did years ago. “We have enough inventory for the next three to four years,” said Cordova.

For prospective sellers? Cordova suggests considering renting their condo out for the time being. “If you really need to sell, the strategy is to list it lower than anybody else in the same building. It’s not time for you to try to experiment with a higher price,” she said.

Today’s home prices and mortgage rates have made it harder for first-time homebuyers to enter the market, reducing demand for condos, which have traditionally served as an entry point to homeownership. The weekly average mortgage rate is 7.18% as of November 5, and home prices remain near historic highs.

Sellers are increasingly dropping their asking prices to attract buyers, Redfin data shows. About 37% of condos that sold in September had a price reduction, compared with 16% during the pandemic-era housing boom five years earlier.

The typical condo that sold in September spent 63 days on the market before going under contract, up from 35 days five years earlier.

Condo buyers in Texas had more negotiating powers than anywhere else in the country in September. San Antonio had the widest seller-buyer gap among the 50 most populous U.S. metro areas, with an estimated 459% more sellers than buyers. Houston followed with 322% more sellers than buyers, then came Austin, with 258% more.

Miami also had one of the nation’s widest gaps, with an estimated 274% more condo sellers than buyers in September, ranking third.

“San Antonio condos may have an attractive purchase price, but buyers are looking at the total monthly payment. Because we have so many affordable single-family homes available, many buyers choose a house when the payments are close,” said Derrell Skillman, a Redfin Premier agent in San Antonio, TX.

Skillman added San Antonio has a large military community, and its housing market tends to follow the military’s permanent change-of-station (PCS) season.

“We usually see more military-related activity during the spring and summer when families are transferring in and out of the area. Once that period slows down, the drop in demand can be more noticeable, especially in a smaller segment like condos,” he said. “HOA costs, financing restrictions, builder incentives, softer investor demand and the slowdown after the military PCS season are all contributing to the buyer’s market for condos,” said Skillman.

Meanwhile, new construction is adding to the softness in Sun Belt markets. The Sun Belt is home to nine of the 10 metros where condo sellers outnumber buyers most.

Nassau County, NY, was the only metro area among the 50 analyzed that was a seller’s market for condos in September. There were 32% fewer sellers than buyers, with an estimated 1,545 buyers compared with 1,052 sellers.

“Condo buyers are mostly people who want maintenance-free living,” said William Ortiz, a Redfin Premier agent in Long Island, NY. “They want to have an easier lifestyle as opposed to all the maintenance and upkeep that comes with a house. Common charges are expensive, but they’re willing to pay for that convenience.”

According to Sandra Glaser, a Redfin senior agent in Long Island, condo demand is coming from three main groups: first-time buyers priced out of single-family homes, particularly those moving from the city to the suburbs; downsizers seeking less upkeep; and investors looking for rental income from properties that require minimal maintenance.

Five other metros — Chicago, Milwaukee, New Brunswick, Newark and San Francisco — had balanced condo markets, where the difference between buyers and sellers was within 10%.

San Francisco’s condo market, for example, is heating up, with the median sale price jumping 20% year over year to nearly $1.2 million in September. Khan, the Redfin senior economist, ties the area’s renewed housing demand to the AI boom, which is attracting highly paid workers and fueling homebuying.

As a senior data reporter, Dian uses data and quantitative analysis to tell stories about economy and real estate. She also builds databases and creates statistical charts that visualize data trends. Previously, Dian spent seven years as a senior data reporter at USA TODAY.

Asad Khan studies housing market trends and the forces behind them as a senior economist at Redfin. Previously, he was an economic consultant at Analysis Group where he worked on antitrust and valuation matters. Asad taught urban economics as a Research Fellow at the University of Wisconsin-Madison and earned a PhD in economics from the University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign. His research has focused on the economics of city structure and zoning policy.

The Best Low-Volume Ski Boots for Narrow Feet, Tested

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

Six narrow-last boots for slim feet, low insteps, and skinny calves, from Lange, Salomon, Nordica, Rossignol, and Tecnica. (Photo: Keri Bascetta)

Overview: The best ski boots for narrow feet are low-volume models with lasts of 97 to 98 mm. Our picks are the Lange Shadow LV, Lange Code 130 LV BOA, Salomon S/Pro Alpha C BOA, Nordica Promachine 3, Rossignol Hi-Speed Elite LV, and Tecnica Mach1 LV 130, with most priced between $550 and $950.

Skiers with narrow feet have a unique problem when it comes to shopping for ski boots. While bootfitters can easily grind, punch, and stretch shells and liners to better accommodate wide feet, it’s more difficult for them to fill up space in a boot to get a narrow foot and heel to stay put. That’s why getting the fit right off the wall matters even more for skiers with narrow feet.

If your feet, ankle, heel, and calf are slim—or your instep low—chances are you’re a candidate for a low-volume boot with a last of around 98 millimeters. It used to be that only a handful of brands made low-volume boots that weren’t the vice grips racers wear on the World Cup circuit. Fortunately, that’s changed. These days, skiers with narrow feet have an abundance of low-volume, narrow-lasted options across flex ranges. Here are the picks our boot testers recommend for 2027.

Last: 97 mm Flexes: 140, 130, 120, 110 (men’s); 115, 95 (women’s) Sizes: 24.5–31.5

Pros and Cons + Extremely responsive flex + Snug heel pockets for high-performance fit – Generic Velcro power strap may not be strong enough for expert skiers

The Shadow series replaced Lange’s long-running RX line, and the payoff for narrow feet is in the fit. If you felt the old RX heel was too wide and blocky, the Shadow LV’s snugged-up heel pocket will put a smile on your face. The forefoot is winnowed down, and the medial wall skips the RX’s familiar navicular and ankle-bone pressure. The instep stays generous, but the liner’s auxetic shaping and reduced material over the cuneiform bones kept us secure without feeling like we were swimming. On snow, the Suspension Blade (a boltless, overlapping spine) and the Dual Pivot send force through the sole to the tip of the ski. The flex curve is much steeper than the RX’s long, slow one: almost any pressure on the tongue drives the shovel, and a few of us found it almost twitchy on a 70-millimter-waisted cheater slalom ski. In longer turns it felt plush and powerful, with resounding feedback in deep powder. Our one gripe: a generic Velcro power strap on a boot this luxe.

Last: 98 mm Flexes: 130, 110 (men’s); 115, 95, 85 (women’s) Sizes: 22–30.5

Pros and Cons + Much easier entry than previous narrow model + Very snug but well-contoured low-volume fit + Highly tactile edging feel and responsiveness – Long-travel flex feel may take getting used to

The all-new Salomon S/Pro Alpha C BOA is a tight boot—old-school narrow—but shaped in a modern way that accounts for the foot’s natural curves and bony spots rather than fighting them. Testers were struck by how easily the foot slid into such a snug shell, unusually easy entry and exit for a narrow-fitting boot, and they loved the fit and feel of the cuff-only BOA closure. But the most notable feature of this boot is the new shell design: it lacks a traditional cuff attachment and pivot point on the inner ankle. The cuff curves smoothly around the ankle with no screws or rivets in sight and anchors low near the sole with two screws and a small metal plate called Powerlink. That low connection creates a direct line from leg to ski edge, with a longer flex feel that took some of us a bit to adjust to. Once we did, we were won over by the connection to the edge and the sensitivity underfoot at any speed.

Last: 98 mm Flexes: 130, 120 (men’s); 115, 95 (women’s) Sizes: 22.5–30.5

Pros and Cons + New 3Force shell is lighter and stiffer for better responsiveness + New lace-up liner improves fit for slender legs + Still one of the tightest fits but avoids hotspots – Lace-up liner makes entry more difficult

The Promachine was the last of the Nordica Machine family to get a “3,” probably because the old boot didn’t need fixing. For low-volume skiers who want more performance, the wait paid off. The new 3Force Frame—a light, stiff skeleton co-molded into the softer polyurethane of the lower shell—sharpens energy transfer and cuts weight, while the shell shape barely changed. That’s good news if you have narrow feet: with its 98-millimeter last, this is still one of the snuggest all-mountain boots on the market, and we were impressed by how free of hotspots the fit stays. The 130-flex model also gets a lace-up version of the cork-lined liner that tightens the instep and lower leg. We loved the extra containment—less so the harder entry—but for slender-legged skiers chasing a gap-free fit, it’s worth the hassle.

Last: 98 mm Flexes: 140, 120 Sizes: 24.5–31.5

Pros and Cons + Precise fit and feel matches on-snow experience + More snug fit throughout than previous version + Updated liner offers less pack-out – Only available in two flex models

The Rossignol Hi-Speed Elite LV isn’t an all-new boot, but a few key updates make it a better one—and a better fit for narrow feet. The 140 flex we tested (a 120 is also available) is stiffer than before, but the real gains come from the new R-Go Fit liner. Different foams around the heel and ankle give more sculpted grip and a 15 percent cut in pack-out where it matters most. A thicker, more textured sock-side material kept us firmly in place without the slippery feel of past versions. A thin, rubberized polyurethane film on the liner’s exterior also helps it mold tighter to the shell. It might sound like liner-ado about nothing, but several of us said it made a bigger difference than most full shell redesigns we test in a year. We called it the perfect narrow boot: firm, snug, and consistent in feel, flex, and power. What you feel at try-on matches what you get on snow, no molding session required.

Last: 97 mm (also 100 mm) Flex: 140, 130, 120 (men’s); 115, 95 (women’s) Sizes: 23.5-29.5

Pros and Cons + Excellent low-volume fit + Damp and powerful + Responsive flex shines in both the resort and the backcountry – Heavy, with poor range of motion

The Lange Code 130 LV is a true low-volume boot in an 80/20 package: a boot worn inbounds most of the time that can sneak out a gate, skin back to the base area, or, frankly, be a little more comfortable on the dance floor. It replaces the much-loved XT3, and the narrow fit is the headline—the anatomical liner provided stellar heel hold and really locked our tester’s foot in. Don’t knock its skiing chops, though. He reported top-tier precision on piste, thanks (he thinks) to the Active Blade in the spine, which made the boot feel almost like it had a bolted spine rather than a walk-mode lever. Dual Core plastic delivered the classic Lange damping, especially on a walk outside Kirkwood where spring powder had set up sooner than he anticipated. The trade-off: a 1,910-gram boot with 54 degrees of range of motion isn’t the best bet for climbing 6,000 vertical feet or banging out lap after lap in a secret stash. But for skiers who want one narrow boot to ski a few laps out of bounds and hammer bumps or groomers all day, the Code is an excellent bet.

Last: 98 mm Flex: 130, 120 (men’s)’ 115, 95 (women’s) Sizes: 22.5–30.5

Pros and Cons + More room in toebox + Snappy rebound + Improved frontside performance – Not as damp as previous version

The previous Mach1 LV was the lowest-volume, 98mm-lasted boot on the market, a favorite of expert skiers with narrow feet who didn’t mind some time in the boot shop. The newest update still has a low-volume, 98-millimeter last, but it’s the most forgiving fit here. Our tester, who has logged well over 300 days in the old boot, says Tecnica raised the toebox ceiling—the old one left his big toe unable to move, even with the thinnest sock—and opened the heel pocket a fair bit wider below the Achilles. He felt no heel movement to speak of, and the stock liner is still one of the best on the market for fit and ease of customization. The new T-Drive and carbon-coinjected cuff give a snappier rebound from the bottom of the flex, and on his “janky kitchen scale,” the boot weighs almost 100 grams less than the previous model. The trade-off is damping: it’s palpably less damp off-piste than the old Mach1. He still calls it a best-in-class all-mountain boot, just one with a balance shifted toward the frontside.

Fit makes or breaks a ski boot, and narrow feet make it harder to get right, because a boot that’s too roomy is tough to fix. Expert skiers vetted and tested every boot here, but the best one for you depends on your own feet. A professional bootfitter is still the fastest way to narrow the field. Until you get there, here’s what to know.

Boot width is listed as the last: the internal width at the forefoot, in millimeters, measured on a size 26/26.5 shell. The number also hints at overall volume—the height over your instep and the room around your lower leg in the cuff. Narrower boots generally have a lower instep and a snugger cuff, while wider boots have a higher instep and a roomier cuff. Boots sort into three groups:

Every boot in this guide is a low-volume model, with lasts of 97 to 98mm.

Not always. Some skiers with narrower feet are comfortable in a medium-volume boot. A bootfitter can stretch a shell more easily than fill one, though, so a boot that’s too roomy is harder to fix than one that’s too snug. Everyone’s tolerance for a snug fit differs, so try several widths at a ski shop and go with what your feet tell you.

Feet and legs come in too many shapes for one tester to speak for everyone. A boot that feels custom-made on one skier can feel punishing on another. That’s why we partner with professional bootfitter Mark Elling, who leads the Masterfit/America’s Best Bootfitters annual ski boot test. Every brand, large or small, is invited to submit models. Over two weeks, 10 expert testers—evenly split between men and women—skied 50 boots, averaging three runs per boot across a range of conditions. For this guide, we pulled the low-volume boots with lasts under 100mm from SKI’s tests and reviews.

Mark Elling has worked full-time in the ski industry for more than 35 years as a writer, a level 3 PSIA-certified instructor, a snowcat skiing guide and a professional bootfitter. He runs a year-round specialty bootfitting lab in Bend, Oregon (BootRX.com), and is a former board-certified pedorthist. Elling coordinates Masterfit’s annual boot test, and he’s the author of The All-Mountain Skier: The Way to Expert Skiing.

21 Easy Play Ideas for Kids (Whether You Have 5, 15, or 30 Minutes)

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Between daily parenting duties, work, and navigating the current world in general, saying your days are full is probably an understatement. And while your kid’s day may be full of exploration and creativity, they can also experience a lot of anxiety, stress, and pressure. That makes it even more important to have some positive, uninterrupted time to reconnect.

Even five minutes can make a difference in a child’s emotional well-being. But as Randy McCoy, VP of Product & Curriculum for The Little Gym, reminds us, how long you play is less important than how often. “It’s not necessarily the ‘duration of play’ but the ‘frequency of play’ that matters most,” Mccoy says. “If a parent can give their child their undivided attention and play with them just five minutes a day, every day, they’re on the right track.”

The good news is, there are lots of ways to play that take next-to-no prep. Read on for 21 easy ideas to play with your kids, whether you have five minutes, 15, or more.

When you have a few minutes, whether it’s before school or after dinner, bust out these little-to-no-prep basics you can use just about anywhere.

1. Try out “Would You Rather?” kid edition. Spend a fun five minutes taking turns asking questions like, “Would you rather never eat ice cream again or get a trampoline?” or “Ride a dinosaur for 20 miles or an elephant for an hour?” The sillier the better. This also makes a great road trip game.

2. Make a dictionary dash. Grab a dictionary and choose a word at random. Read it out loud and have the kids guess what it means. If they can read, they can take turns finding words and trying to stump you.

3. Tickle your sidekick’s funny bone. Whip out brain busters or riddles that will make them laugh. Luckily, you don’t need Comedy Central writers for this one. We think your goofballs will get a kick out of our ultimate list of jokes for kids.

4. A few rounds of “We’re going on a picnic” alphabet-style will entertain the tot lot. The first player up says, “I’m going on a picnic,” then adds a tasty packable to the phrase, like an apple, apricot, or avocado. Stick with ABC order or focus on just one letter for each round.

5. Play 20 questions. Have your kids guess what you’re thinking of, and keep it simple, like something you can see from where you’re standing.

6. Play a classic game of Rock, Paper, Scissors—Japanese style. It’s called Janken. Start by saying “saisho wa guu.” Then “janken pon” as you pump, before throwing on “pon.” The winning combos are still the same in this timeless, quick game you can play at home or on the go.

7. Turn yourself into a living “spot the difference” puzzle. Start with a slow model turn, then disappear and make a quick change out of sight. When you come back, your cutie’s got to spot what’s different. Did you take off a cap? Put on a scarf? Untie a shoe? Keep it easy for the tots, and kick it up a notch for older kiddos (only one earring!). Then let them have a turn trying to stump you.

8. Try out a sequencing game when the wanderlust takes hold. Name a person, place, and object, all starting with the same letter of the alphabet, then string them together like kids lining up for the school bus. “Alice from Australia loves alligators!” See how far you can get in a short amount of time.

For parents, playing with our kids allows us to step out of all those daily parental duties and, as McCoy explains, build friendship between parent and child. “It provides an opportunity for you to interact with them on a different level,” he says. “For a special moment, you step out of the ‘parent’ role and step into a playmate role.”  Take a break from your to-do list and try one of these 15-minute ideas that will thrill the kids and relax you, too.

9. Post-It style. A stack of Post-It notes and a pen are all it takes to play this short-and-sweet variation of the classic guessing game for parties. Choose someone to be “it.” Write on the Post-It note the name of a character from a book, movie, or show that your kid will recognize—it can be a famous person, someone they know, or a fictional character. Stick the note to their forehead, without letting them see what you wrote. Now take turns going around the room giving “it” clues to help them guess who is on their head.

10. Curl up with a good book. Putting out a basket of books in a central location motivates the kids to take a look through the pages when they’ve got time to spare. Add in a sweet bookmark that keeps track of time to make it count.

11. Give them their daily feels. Keep your favorite sensory bin fillers on hand (like cotton balls and dry beans), along with trinkets to bury and cups for spilling and filling. When you’ve got a short window, pull out the bin, fill ‘er up, and watch your kids dig and drizzle until it’s time to go.

12. Set up a quick cotton ball and straw race. Perfect for the kitchen table before lunch is served. Have siblings challenge each other for the pole position on a straightaway down the center of the table, or set up a simple obstacle course using cups that the kiddos have to work around. Ready, set, go!

13. Give a penny for their thoughts when they write in a journal. Setting aside a short amount of time to write (each day!) is a great way to encourage your amateur author’s creativity. Use silly or thought-provoking prompts to get them thinking, like, “The first time I tried …” or “When I get to be principal, I’ll …” or even “The craziest thing that happened today…” Write on, brother!

14. Give up the charade. Have your little monkey stay in character when you play a quick game of animal charades. No prep is necessary for this easy guessing game. Just pick your favorite animal and go for it! With each successful guess, trade places and start again. Ee-i-ee-i-o!

Whether your aim is active playtime or quiet(er) crafting time, filling a cool 30 minutes with your kids is easier than you think. It may take a bit of planning, but once the prep is done, it’s a sit-back-and-relax situation that’s well worth the time upfront.

15. All things LEGO. If LEGO was the first thing that popped into your head, then we’re right there with you. They’re the forever fallback for a reason, but to hit that half-hour sweet spot, try throwing a LEGO challenge into the mix: building a car that can also work underwater, or constructing a tower that’s at least 24 inches tall. Then let your master-builder go to work.

16. Spin them right round. Ana at Babble Dabble Do has a new way to play with Perler beads. She puts them to work teaching science and playing tricks when these melted beauties become spinning tops. It’s just the right amount of make-and-play to fill a brief window in your busy day.

17. One for the movers and shakers. Print out (and laminate if you’re feeling bold) The Alerting Activity Game, designed by Training Happy Hearts. Keep the deck on hand to stack the odds in your favor when the kids need something to do. With simple activities like “jump up and down ten times” or “pretend to be a volcano,” it’s packed with energy-burning activities that kids can run through on their own or with you!

18. Bring the outside in and paint it. If snow isn’t covering your yard, go for a hunt for leaves, evergreen boughs, and sticks. Then take your findings to the craft table to fancy them up with paint. Acrylics work great for this project.

For activities that span the hour, the two-step Make & Play approach works well. You may want to do a little prep the day before to make it easier, like putting crafting necessities into one small bin that you can pull out to surprise the kids.

19. Wage war. Ah, the classic card game that is so simple to learn and so hard to win. It is possible that a game of war can take longer than one hour, but it rarely takes less. You do two card battles with the highest card the winner until one person has all the cards. Get the complete rules here.

20. Create a mini catapult. Little Bins for Little Hands has an awesome tutorial, using craft sticks and other office supplies you’re sure to have around the house. Once it’s built, set up target practice plates before letting the marshmallows fly.

21. Put on a show. Puppets never get old; they’re easy to make with an old sock, brown paper lunch bag, or even familiar characters glued to craft sticks. Whatever method you choose, spend time creating and playing with puppets when you’ve got an hour or more to spare. Be sure to stock the craft bin with all kinds of googly eyes, buttons, yarn, and doo-dads to help bring your buddies to life. Then find an old sheet or use a baby’s blanket draped over the table to create a stage. Bravo!

Bringing a little more joy to parenthood.

Forget Mosquitoes in Amber. Scientists May Have Found Dinosaur Blood in a 115 Million-Year-Old Bone.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Okay, you can’t clone a raptor from heme. But finding it after 115 million years is almost as wild.

At first glance, the news seems like the opening lines of a Jurassic Park sequel. In a study published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, an international team of scientists reports finding traces of heme, an organic molecule that helps blood carry oxygen, in the vascular canals of a 115-million-year-old dinosaur bone. The fossil comes from the leg of Aratasaurus museunacionali, a distant relative of Velociraptor that lived during the early Cretaceous.

The fossilized bones were found in 2008 at a mine in the Romualdo Formation of northeast Brazil. The potential discovery of molecular evidence of blood inside the bones would be remarkable because, simply put, blood doesn’t usually stick around very long after death.

“Detection of endogenous biomolecules in vertebrate fossils remains controversial because labile compounds degrade quickly and are highly susceptible to contamination,” the authors write in the study. “By identifying heme-derived molecules—key components of blood—and confirming their spatial association, the study reduces the likelihood of contamination.”

Previous claims of fossilized blood cells have mostly stumbled over the same two problems: contamination, and the difficulty of ruling out structures that only look biological. According to Science, at least 14 previous studies have claimed to find evidence of dino blood, but none have been robust enough to convince the scientific field at large, though one paleobiologist, speaking with Science, says this new study is “the most comprehensive effort that’s been done so far.”

The story of this particular fossil is a harrowing one as it is the lucky survivor of a 2018 fire that destroyed the National Museum at the Federal University of Rio de Janeiro. In fact, its scientific name means “the dinosaur born from the National Museum fire.” Although the bones were not particularly well preserved, Curtin University’s Kliti Grice and a team of researchers from the U.S., Japan, and Brazil analyzed the fossils using liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry, Raman spectroscopy, and transmission electron microscopy.

Together, these techniques detected heme (a key component of hemoglobin) inside the bone’s vascular canals, but not in the surrounding rock. That contrast is key, because it suggests the heme came from the dinosaur and didn’t seep in from its surroundings. Cell-like structures in the surrounding sediment, by contrast, were dominated by compounds derived from chlorophyll, a sign of plant or microbial origin.

“A key question was whether the structures we saw were really biological remnants—that is, actual blood—or something that may have happened to the fossil later as the tissues were turning to stone,” Grice wrote in an article for The Conversation. “We evaluated and ruled out several alternative explanations for the structures. In the end, we believe they’re most likely authentic fossilised red blood cell remains.”

But for a substance that’s famously finicky about fossilization, how did evidence of blood remain within these vascular canals for 115 million years? Well, like most ancient bones that survive to this day, it’s all about dying in the right conditions. As this dinosaur traipsed around what’s now northeast Brazil—at a time when the landmass was still physically connected to Africa—it died and sank into a watery, low-oxygen grave where mats of microbes grew over the top. According to Grice, the additional presence of the mineral hausmannite, which can act like an antioxidant, might’ve also slowed down the decay. Eventually, the blood cells mineralized much like the vascular channel and the bones themselves.

“The fossil red blood cell-like structures appear a bit larger and more rounded than the cells found in modern birds and crocodilians,” Grice writes. “It could suggest the red blood cells of dinosaurs not related to birds were shaped differently than those of birds, their modern relatives.” However, North Carolina State University paleontologist Mary Schweitzer tells Science that this difference in blood cell shape—as well as the lack of a nucleus in the cell—is too radical a departure from birds and crocodilians, suggesting these structures aren’t blood cells at all.

Grice says that all indications, whether from microscopy, mineralogy, spectroscopy, or molecular geochemistry, point to the first-ever discovery of molecular evidence of dinosaur blood. But as past studies have shown, it might take more evidence to win over the field at large.

Darren lives in Portland, has a cat, and writes/edits about sci-fi and how our world works. You can find his previous stuff at Gizmodo and Paste if you look hard enough.

I Spent Years Hiding My Ulcerative Colitis. One Night at Sundance Changed How I Saw My Disease

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

In an exclusive excerpt from her new book, NBC correspondent Chloe Melas shares the reality of navigating an early relationship with IBD.

For 15 years, I've battled a form of Inflammatory Bowel Disease (IBD) known as Ulcerative Colitis. It's an autoimmune disease in which your body attacks your colon. When I was diagnosed at 25-years-old after finding blood in my stool, I felt like I had nowhere to turn because neither side of my family has gut issues. I thought if maybe I ignored my symptoms (the blood, the bloating, the constipation), it would eventually go away. It didn't. As I continued to chase my career and search for love, things got worse and I eventually couldn't hide it anymore. When I went to the Sundance Film Festival I was at the height of my disease and I felt like it was following me like a cloud trying to rain on every moment of joy. What follows is a story—from my new book that I co-authored with Judy Batalion, The Girl’s Guide to Guts—that I never planned to share publicly. But I am now in hopes of helping anyone out there feel less alone. — Chloe Melas

As I rose up through the TV ranks, my invitations became better and better. I wasn’t at the end of the red carpets anymore; I was now just a few spots from the end! One of the best parts of the job is being able to attend some of the most exclusive events, like the Golden Globe Awards, private concerts, and the annual Sundance Film Festival, which, until 2026, was held at the foot of the Rocky Mountains in Park City, Utah. Sundance, started by the late actor Robert Redford, is a week-long party celebrating the best in film from that year, with a who’s who of Hollywood descending on this snowy, storybook town.

In 2012, worlds collided when (my now-husband) Brian and I first started dating, because he coincidentally hosted the hardest-to-get-into party at Sundance. Just a few months into dating, he invited me to come along. I said yes before even checking to see if I had enough vacation days. Somehow I convinced my editor that I should go and cover all the celebrity happenings. I also told her it would cost the company nothing because I had been invited by a film studio, which was a total lie—I put it all on my credit card and told myself I’d figure it out later. Luckily, she said yes!

Brian and I had already been on a few trips, but it was getting harder to pretend I was headed to the gym for a workout when I really was going to find a lobby loo. Eventually I had to come clean, because saying I was going to hit the gym at 11 p.m. after a couple of cocktails wasn’t passing his BS meter.

But the good news was, my flares were intermittent. And because I had a really good poker face, he had no clue just how bad things could get. Plus, over the eighteen months since my symptoms first wreaked havoc on my life, I’d become a pro on how to mentally map out bathrooms wherever we went. I’d also order sparingly at dinner. I didn’t want to be holed up in some bathroom while he was out there wondering what he’d gotten himself into. Trying to hide my disease while also managing my day-to-day emotional spirals, however, became a lot to handle.

We finally made it to Park City, and from our balcony we could see the snow-covered mountains in the distance and the beautifully dressed celebrities in their winter wear dotting the streets below us. I couldn’t wait to wear an outfit I had been saving in my suitcase—the all-white look. We’re talking a faux fur vest and matching beanie, topped off with the cutest low-rise skinny jeans. I looked like an influencer before there even were influencers, y’all!

As Brian and I made our way to a rooftop party, I suddenly felt the urge. My eyes immediately darted to a hallway on the other side of these massive sliding glass doors. It was a single-stall bathroom and the line was too long. I had to make a move, and fast. I tugged on Brian’s sweater and whispered, “I need to run back to the hotel.” He understood it was urgent code for needing to use the bathroom. He handed me our room key, and I couldn’t have left any faster.

As I started speed-walking toward our hotel, the soft buzz of the alcohol slowly began to dissipate, and my intermittent depression began to creep in. Here we were, in this beautiful city at a dreamlike event, and it had been interrupted once again by my disease. Can’t I catch a break? I thought.

As I crossed the street to our hotel, the need to go was only intensifying. Almost there, I said to myself. Squeeze your cheeks!

I got to the elevator bank and pushed the up button several times, as if that would make the elevator move faster. I painfully watched the numbers click at a glacial pace from floor to floor: eight . . . seven . . . six . . . And then, like a volcano about to erupt, my poor body just couldn’t keep it in. I prayed it would just be some flatulence.

Luckily, no one was around, but I was devastated, embarrassed, and defeated. When I got back to the hotel room I threw away those new, gorgeous jeans that now represented something so painful. I sat and cried.

“How much longer can I live like this?” I whimpered.

I didn’t feel like going back out. I had worked too hard to prevent ulcerative colitis from stealing my joy and taking over my life. That day, I had lost the battle. I had assumed that if I could map bathrooms in every space I entered, limit what I ate, and take my medicine, situations like this wouldn’t happen. So much of ulcerative colitis was accepting that most of this was totally out of my control. I texted Brian that I was going to skip the rest of the events that afternoon. He came back and just held me on the bed.

This was the only time I ever soiled myself. But I can’t tell you the countless times I’ve busted into coffee shops and restaurants to beg for the code to the bathroom only to be told I’d need to wait in line and buy something.

They say you’re supposed to know your nearest exit. But as someone living with bowel disease, I’ve always scouted the nearest bathroom. If I was going to be traveling, I’d time my meals so that I could avoid having to make a pit stop on long car rides, train trips, or flights. I’d even get dehydrated, because I’d be so nervous that even water would trigger me. I was withering away both physically and emotionally.

It sounds dramatic, and that’s because it is. But I have managed to find the humor. I can even laugh (a little) now at what happened at Sundance and say to myself, Well, at least Brad Pitt wasn’t standing in the elevator next to me.

Nissan Dusts Off the ‘Patrol’ Badge for America With a New Off-Road Armada

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Granted, the Armada Patrol Pro-4X is largely new in name alone, but we’re just happy to see that name back on our shores.

Nissan’s vehicles may not always come up in the top spot in comparison tests, but there’s one arena where the brand indisputably competes at the top rung in the United States: strong names. Pathfinder, Titan, Frontier, Rogue all ooze the toughness and attitude you want to identify with a truck or SUV. Leaf makes an electric vehicle’s eco-friendliness clear with a single syllable. Even Murano—named after fancy Italian glassware—suggests premium elegance in a way that, say, “RX450+” doesn’t.

And the 2027 Nissan Armada Patrol Pro-4X’s name makes it pretty, pretty clear that the company doesn’t have any intention of changing course anytime soon.

The new-for-’27 moniker marks the return of the “Patrol” badge to Nissan’s U.S. lineup after a 66-year absence, the nameplate last seeing the side of a vehicle in an American showroom the same year Neil Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin planted their boots on the moon. It’s remained in widespread use elsewhere on Planet Earth, however, on several generations of burly body-on-frame sport-utility vehicles, including the one sold here as the Armada. That may mean the Armada Patrol name is sourced from the Department of Redundancy Department, but hey, we’re still happy to see it back.

Most of the rest of the SUV will be familiar to anyone who’s experienced the previous Armada Pro-4X off-road trim, as the new version is effectively a rebrand of that, but there are a couple other tweaks made for the 2027 model year. There’s a Nismo roof rack and Nismo rock sliders, as well as available Nismo Axis wheels wrapped in BFGoodrich off-road tires.

Otherwise, the new trim comes packing all the regular goodies previously found on a factory go-anywhere Armada. There’s the 3.5-liter twin-turbo V-6 making 425 horsepower and 516 pound-feet of torque, connected to a four-wheel drive system with electronic limited-slip differential and a 9-speed automatic gearbox. Adaptive air suspension, skid plate, off-road cameras and all-terrain tires all help it push further past the pavement than regular Armadas, while fender flares and red accents help set it apart visually.

Should you be interested in taking the 2027 Armada Patrol Pro-4x home—ideally by driving across a mountain range or two—you should plan on forking over at least $81,265 for the privilege. That’s $11,700 more than the starting price of a basic four-wheel drive Armada, but a few grand cheaper than its key rivals, the Ford Expedition Tremor and Chevrolet Tahoe Z71 with the 6.2-liter V-8. Also, with Detroit’s models, you can’t stand on the running board and make dad jokes about being “on patrol.”

Will Sabel Courtney is a contributing editor at Road & Track. A 12-year veteran of journalism, he formerly worked at Gear Patrol, The Drive, and RIDES Magazine, and contributed to the New York Daily News and Jalopnik, among other outlets. You can often find him test-driving new cars in New York City, cursing the slow-moving traffic surrounding him.
